By taking appropriate differences of the marginal treatment response functions, I obtain identification of the marginal controlled spillover effect (MCSE) and the marginal controlled direct effect (MCDE).

Proof.

See Appendix
J
.
∎

6
Conclusion

This paper develops a general framework for identifying causal effects in environments with within-group spillovers and endogenous treatment decisions. By relaxing the Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption (SUTVA), the framework accommodates settings in which an individual’s outcome depends not only on her own treatment but also on the treatment selection of group members. It further allows each individual’s treatment decision to depend on instruments assigned to other group members.

The paper introduces two classes of causal parameters, he generalized local average controlled spillover and direct effects (LACSEs and LACDEs) and the marginal controlled spillover and direct effects (MCSEs and MCDEs), which extend the standard local average and marginal treatment effect frameworks to settings with spillovers. The LACSEs and LACDEs quantify peer and own treatment effects for specific subpopulations, while the MCSEs and MCDEs capture these effects conditional on continuous values of unobserved heterogeneity within groups. The paper formally establishes general conditions for the point identification of the LACSEs and LACDEs, characterizing the instrumental variation necessary for identification regardless of whether the instruments are discrete or continuous. It also shows that the MCSEs and MCDEs are nonparametrically point identified from continuous instrumental variation without imposing functional form restrictions on the outcome equation or on the joint distribution of unobserved characteristics within the group, thereby accommodating flexible forms of spillover structures.

These results extend existing approaches to causal inference with spillovers, showing that the MCSE-MCDE framework provides a natural generalization of the standard marginal treatment effect (MTE) model to spillover settings. Furthermore, the paper establishes that these marginal controlled effects serve as building blocks for policy-relevant treatment parameters (PRTEs), enabling the evaluation of both direct and spillover effects under counterfactual policy interventions.

For estimation and inference, the paper develops a semiparametric estimation strategy that builds on
13
, extending it to accommodate within-group spillovers while mitigating the curse of dimensionality associated with covariates. Asymptotic properties of the semiparametric estimators are derived, and a parametric estimation framework is proposed as a practical complement when sample sizes are limited or group sizes are large. Monte Carlo simulations demonstrate that the parametric estimators perform well in finite samples, providing accurate estimates and confidence interval coverage.

An empirical application using the National Longitudinal Study of Adolescent to Adult Health (Add Health) illustrates the framework’s practical relevance. The analysis examines how education attainment affects long-term earnings within best-friend networks. The results indicate positive dependence between friends’ unobserved characteristics and reveal heterogeneous spillover patterns, showing that both the magnitude and direction of peer influences vary with individuals’ and their best friends’ education attainment.

Finally, the paper extends the framework to settings with exposure mappings, where outcomes depend on a known function of group members’ treatments rather than the full treatment vector. This generalization broadens the applicability of the framework to environments with varying or large group sizes, while preserving nonparametric point identification under continuous instrumental variation. The appendix further extends the analysis to settings with continuous endogenous treatments and establishes point identification results under continuous instruments.

Overall, the proposed framework offers an econometric foundation for identifying and estimating causal effects in the presence of within-group spillovers and endogenous treatments. It provides theoretical and practical tools for studying a wide range of social, education, and economic interactions. Future research could extend the framework by modeling endogenous group formation, and by developing more efficient semiparametric estimation methods to enhance finite-sample performance.

Appendix A
Further Details of the Modeling Framework

A.1
Endogenous Effects in the Outcome

Some studies (e.g.,
9
) model spillover effects using a system of structural equations in which a unit’s outcome may directly depend on the outcomes of their peers. When outcomes among group members influence one another, such interactions are typically referred to as endogenous effects. In contrast, this framework does not explicitly model endogenous effects, since
Y_{i}
does not directly depend on the outcomes of unit
i
’s peers,
Y_{-i}
. However, this should not be interpreted as ruling out the possibility of endogenous effects within the model. As discussed in
29
, the potential outcome
Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime})
can be interpreted as a reduced-form solution of underlying structural models that include endogenous effects.

For illustration, consider a system of linear structural equations for
Y_{i}
, where
i\in\{0,1\}
, within group
g
. The group subscript
g
is omitted for notational simplicity.

\displaystyle Y_{0}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}D_{0}+\alpha_{2}D_{1}+\alpha_{3}Y_{1}+U_{0}+\gamma_{1}U_{1},

\displaystyle Y_{1}=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}D_{1}+\beta_{2}D_{0}+\beta_{3}Y_{0}+U_{1}+\gamma_{2}U_{0},\alpha_{3}\beta_{3}\neq 1

where
D_{i}
denotes unit
i
’s treatment and
U_{i}
captures unobserved factors. One can solve the system to obtain a reduced-form expression for
Y_{i}
as a linear function of
D_{i}
and
D_{-i}
, without explicitly involving
Y_{-i}
:

\displaystyle Y_{0}=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{3}\beta_{0}+\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}\beta_{2}\right)D_{0}+\left(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}\beta_{1}\right)D_{1}}{1-\alpha_{3}\beta_{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(1+\alpha_{3}\gamma_{2}\right)U_{0}+\left(\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{3}\right)U_{1}}{1-\alpha_{3}\beta_{3}},

\displaystyle Y_{1g}=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{0}+\beta_{3}\alpha_{0}+\left(\beta_{1}+\beta_{3}\alpha_{2}\right)D_{1}+\left(\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}\alpha_{1}\right)D_{0}}{1-\alpha_{3}\beta_{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(1+\beta_{3}\gamma_{1}\right)U_{1}+\left(\beta_{3}+\gamma_{2}\right)U_{0}}{1-\alpha_{3}\beta_{3}}.

Therefore,
Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime})
can be interpreted as solutions for
Y_{i}
when the treatment assignments are set to
D_{i}=d
and
D_{-i}=d^{\prime}
. This interpretation remains valid when the structural functions are nonlinear. In the nonlinear case, the link between the potential outcome equations and the underlying structural models is less transparent, though it can still be derived by researchers in the context of specific applications. In this paper, however, the focus is on the reduced-form treatment effects of a unit’s’ own and peers’ treatments, rather than on the structural parameters embedded in the structural equations.

A.2
Simultaneous Incomplete Information Game

In this framework, unit
i
’s treatment decision does not directly depend on her peers’ treatment choices
D_{-i}
, implying the absence of strategic interaction in treatment take-up. This structure is consistent with a simultaneous incomplete information game, as analyzed in
1
. In such a framework,
V_{i}
represents private information observed only by unit
i
, while
Z=(Z_{i},Z_{-i})
is a vector of public signals observed by all group members. Each unit
i
forms a subjective belief about the joint distribution
P_{i}(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid Z)
, and makes an optimal decision accordingly. The decision rule for unit
i
can be derived as

D_{i}=\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq\alpha_{i}\underbrace{P_{i}\big(D_{-i}=1\mid D_{i}=1,Z\big)}_{\text{Unit i's belief, function of }Z}\}.

The optimal decision function satisfies the single threshold crossing structure imposed in our setting, with unit
i
’s belief captured by the threshold function
h_{i}(Z)
.
1
provides conditions for the existence and uniqueness of equilibrium beliefs. For a detailed discussion of the simultaneous-move game of incomplete information, see
1
.

In contrast, other studies, such as
3
and
21
, focus on settings with direct strategic interactions between
D_{i}
and
D_{-i}
. However, those models do not allow unit
i
’s treatment to be directly influenced by peer instruments
Z_{-i}
. Both incomplete information games and models with strategic interaction are empirically relevant, and their associated identification strategies can be viewed as complementary to the framework developed in this paper.

Appendix B
Proof of Identification for LACSEs and LACDEs

Identifying the generalized LACSEs:
Suppose that two distinct pairs of propensity scores,
(p_{0},p_{1})
and
(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime})
, exist in
\mathcal{P}
with
p_{1}^{\prime}>p_{1}
. The following discussion illustrates how this variation can be exploited to identify the local average controlled spillover and direct effects described in Item 1 of Theorem
1
.

Given
(p_{0},p_{1})\in\mathcal{P}
, consider the observed conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
. This expectation identifies the average potential outcome
Y_{i}(1,1)
for the subpopulation whose unobserved characteristics lie in the region
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1,1)\mathbbm{1}\left\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\right\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1,1)\mathbbm{1}\left\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\right\}\big],

where the second equality holds because the propensity scores are functions of the instrumental variables and are therefore independent of the potential outcomes and unobservables
(V_{i},V_{-i})
, as implied by Assumption
1
. Similarly, evaluating
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid\cdot,\cdot]
at
(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime})
identifies the mean potential outcome
Y_{i}(1,1)
for the subpopulation with
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
. The difference between this and the expectation evaluated at
(p_{0},p_{1})
identifies the average potential outcome
Y_{i}(1,1)
for the subpopulation characterized by
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

(12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1,1)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}\big],

which implies that the subpopulation characterized by
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
switches to the treatment combination
(D_{i},D_{-i})=(1,1)
when the peer’s propensity score increases from
p_{1}
to
p_{1}^{\prime}
, holding unit
i
’s score fixed at
p_{0}
.

Applying the same logic to the conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}D_{i}(1-D_{-i})\mid\cdot,\cdot]
evaluated at
(p_{0},p_{1})
and
(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime})
yields the negative of the average potential outcome
Y_{i}(1,0)
for the same subpopulation,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

(13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1,0)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}\big],

indicating that these units transition away from the treatment combination
(D_{i},D_{-i})=(1,0)
under this change in propensity scores.

Summing Equations (
12
) and (
13
) identifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big(Y_{i}(1,1)-Y_{i}(1,0)\big)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}\big],

which is an average spillover effect for the subpopulation
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
, holding unit
i
’s treatment fixed at
D_{i}=1
. Additionally, the share of the subpopulation
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
can be identified from

\mathbb{E}\big[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big],

allowing the local average controlled spillover effect
\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(1)}(V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime})
to be identified as

\frac{\mu_{i,i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-\mu_{i,i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)}{C\left(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-C\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)}=\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(1)}(V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}),

where
\mu_{i,i}^{(d)}(p_{0},p_{1})\equiv\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
,
d\in\{0,1\}
, and
C(p_{0},p_{1})=\mathbb{E}[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
.

Replacing
D_{i}
with
(1-D_{i})
in Equations (
12
) and (
13
) and repeating the same steps yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big(Y_{i}(0,1)-Y_{i}(0,0)\big)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}>p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}\big],

which identifies the average spillover effect for the subpopulation
\{V_{i}>p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
, holding unit
i
’s treatment fixed at
D_{i}=0
. Dividing by the proportion of the subpopulation, which is identified from

\big(p_{1}^{\prime}-p_{1}\big)-\big[C(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime})-C(p_{0},p_{1})\big]=\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}>p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\big),

then the local average controlled spillover effect
\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(0)}(V_{i}>p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime})
can be identified as

\frac{\mu_{i,i}^{(0)}\left(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-\mu_{i,i}^{(0)}\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)}{\left(p_{1}^{\prime}-p_{1}\right)-\left[C\left(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-C\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)\right]}=\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(0)}(V_{i}>p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}).

Identifying the generalized LACDEs:
Suppose there exist two pairs of propensity scores,
(p_{0},p_{1})
and
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})
, with
p_{0}^{\prime}>p_{0}
. The difference in the observed conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid\cdot,\cdot]
evaluated at these two points identifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

(14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1,1)\mathbbm{1}\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}\big],

indicating that the subpopulation with unobserved characteristics
\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}
transitions to the treatment configuration
(D_{i},D_{-i})=(1,1)
when the propensity scores shift from
(p_{0},p_{1})
to
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})
. Similarly, the difference in conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})D_{-i}\mid\cdot,\cdot]
evaluated at
(p_{0},p_{1})
and
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})
identifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

(15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(0,1)\mathbbm{1}\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}\big],

implying that the same subpopulation moves away from the treatment configuration
(D_{i},D_{-i})=(0,1)
under this change in the propensity scores.

Summing Equations (
14
) and (
15
) identifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big(Y_{i}(1,1)-Y_{i}(0,1)\big)\mathbbm{1}\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}\big],

which is the average direct effect for the subpopulation
\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}
, holding the peer’s treatment fixed at
D_{-i}=1
. The share of this subpopulation can be identified from

\mathbb{E}\big[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big],

so the local average controlled direct effect
\operatorname{LACDE}_{i}^{(1)}(p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}\leq p_{1})
is given by

\frac{\mu_{i,-i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}\right)-\mu_{i,-i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)}{C\left(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}\right)-C\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)}=\operatorname{LACDE}_{i}^{(1)}(p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}),

where
\mu_{i,-i}^{(d)}(p_{0},p_{1})\equiv\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{-i}=d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
, for
d\in\{0,1\}
.

Replacing
D_{-i}
with
(1-D_{-i})
in Equations (
14
) and (
15
) and repeating the same reasoning identifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big(Y_{i}(1,0)-Y_{i}(0,0)\big)\mathbbm{1}\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}>p_{1}\}\big],

which corresponds to the average direct effect for the subpopulation
\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}>p_{1}\}
, holding the peer
-i
’s treatment fixed at
D_{-i}=0
.

Dividing by the fraction of this subpopulation, which is identified as

\big(p_{0}^{\prime}-p_{0}\big)-\big[C(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})-C(p_{0},p_{1})\big]=\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}>p_{1}\big),

yields the local average controlled direct effect

\frac{\mu_{i,-i}^{(0)}(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})-\mu_{i,-i}^{(0)}(p_{0},p_{1})}{\big(p_{0}^{\prime}-p_{0}\big)-\big[C(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})-C(p_{0},p_{1})\big]}=\operatorname{LACDE}_{i}^{(0)}(p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},V_{-i}>p_{1}).

Identifying the generalized LACSEs and LACDEs:
Suppose now that the support of the propensity scores exhibits greater variation, such that four distinct pairs of propensity scores,
(p_{0},p_{1})
,
(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime})
,
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})
, and
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime})
, exist in
\mathcal{P}
with
p_{0}^{\prime}>p_{0}
and
p_{1}^{\prime}>p_{1}
. These variations enable the identification of
\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(d)}(P)
and
\operatorname{LACDE}i^{(d)}(P)
for
d\in{0,1}
, corresponding to the subpopulation with unobserved characteristics in the region
\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
.

For example, applying the identification strategy developed earlier to the points
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})
and
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime})
identifies the average spillover effect for the subpopulation
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
, holding unit
i
’s treatment fixed at
D_{i}=1
, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\left(Y_{i}(1,1)-Y_{i}(1,0)\right)\mathbbm{1}\left\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\right\}\big].

Subtracting the previously identified average spillover effect for the subpopulation
\{V_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
yields

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]\big)

\displaystyle-\big(\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\left(Y_{i}(1,1)-Y_{i}(1,0)\right)\mathbbm{1}\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}\big],

which identifies the average spillover effect for the subpopulation

\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
, holding unit
i
’s treatment fixed at
D_{i}=1
.

Since the proportion of the subpopulation
\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\}
can be identified from

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{E}[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}]-\mathbb{E}[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}^{\prime},P_{-i}=p_{1}]\big)

\displaystyle-\big(\mathbb{E}[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}^{\prime}]-\mathbb{E}[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]\big),

the
\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(1)}\big(p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\big)
is obtained as

\frac{\left[\mu_{i,i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-\mu_{i,i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}\right)\right]-\left[\mu_{i,i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-\mu_{i,i}^{(1)}\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)\right]}{\left[C\left(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-C\left(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}\right)\right]-\left[C\left(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime}\right)-C\left(p_{0},p_{1}\right)\right]},

where
\mu_{i,i}^{(d)}(p_{0},p_{1})\equiv\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
and
C(p_{0},p_{1})\equiv\mathbb{E}[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
.

Applying a similar strategy, the average spillover and direct effects for the subpopulations corresponding to the propensity score pairs
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1})
and
(p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}^{\prime})
can also be identified. By taking differences between the previously identified average effects for the subpopulations associated with
(p_{0},p_{1})
and
(p_{0},p_{1}^{\prime})
, one can identify
\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(d)}(P)
and
\operatorname{LACDE}_{i}^{(d)}(P)
for
d\in\{0,1\}
, where
P=\{p_{0}<V_{i}\leq p_{0}^{\prime},p_{1}<V_{-i}\leq p_{1}^{\prime}\big\}
. These identification results correspond to Item 3 of Theorem
1
.

Appendix C
Local Average Effects with Binary Instruments

This section illustrates that when the instrumental variables exhibit limited variation, additional restrictions are necessary to identify the local average controlled effects described in Theorem
1
.

Consider the case in which the instrument is binary,
Z_{i}\in{0,1}
, for all units
i
across groups. As discussed in Remark
1
, the corresponding propensity scores take on only four possible values. To apply the identification results in Theorem
1
, these propensity scores must satisfy certain equalities. Specifically, identification of LACSEs requires that any two of
P_{i}(0,0)
,
P_{i}(0,1)
,
P_{i}(1,0)
, or
P_{i}(1,1)
be equal, while identification of LACDEs requires that any two of
P_{-i}(0,0)
,
P_{-i}(0,1)
,
P_{-i}(1,0)
, or
P_{-i}(1,1)
be equal.

The
one-sided noncompliance
condition that frequently imposed in the literature is a special case of these requirements. It assumes that no unit takes the treatment unless assigned to it, which translates to

P_{i}(0,0)=P_{i}(0,1)=0,\quad P_{-i}(0,0)=P_{-i}(0,1)=0.

Assume that the propensity scores can be ordered as

P_{i}(0,0)\leq P_{i}(0,1)\leq P_{i}(1,0)\leq P_{i}(1,1),

The four observed pairs of propensity scores corresponding to different instrument assignments are illustrated as black dots in Figure
4
.

Figure 4:
Identifying Local Average Controlled Effects via Binary Instrument

Under one-sided noncompliance, consider first a change in the instrument assignment from
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=(0,0)
to
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=(1,0)
. In this case, unit
i
’s propensity score increases from
P_{i}(0,0)
to
P_{i}(1,0)
, while the peer
-i
’s propensity score remains constant at
P_{-i}(0,0)=P_{-i}(0,1)=0
. According to Item 2 of Theorem
1
, this variation identifies the local average controlled direct effect
\operatorname{LACDE}_{i}^{(1)}(0<V_{i}\leq P_{i}(1,0),0<V_{-i}<1)
, as illustrated by the yellow-shaded area in the left panel of Figure
4
.

Similarly, if the instrument assignment changes from
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=(0,0)
to
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=(0,1)
, the peer
-i
’s propensity score shifts from
P_{-i}(0,0)
to
P_{-i}(1,0)
, while the unit
i
’s propensity score remains constant at
P_{i}(0,0)=P_{i}(0,1)=0
. In this case, Item 1 of Theorem
1
implies identification of the local average controlled spillover effect
\operatorname{LACSE}_{i}^{(0)}(0<V_{i}<1,0<V_{-i}<P_{-i}(1,0))
, as shown by the blue-shaded area in the right panel of Figure
4
. The identified local average controlled spillover and direct effects correspond to the same causal parameters identified in
33
, which studies a similar setting with spillovers in both outcomes and endogenous treatment using a binary instrumental variable.

Without additional equalities among the propensity scores, it is generally difficult to identify the generalized local average controlled spillover and direct effects. The challenge arises because limited variation in the instrumental variables prevents constructing two distinct pairs of propensity scores in which one unit’s propensity score changes while the other’s remains fixed. Such variation is crucial for identification, as the model structure embedded in the treatment selection equation implies a monotonicity condition for each unit’s treatment decision. Specifically, if the propensity scores satisfy

P_{i}(0,0)\leq P_{i}(0,1)\leq P_{i}(1,0)\leq P_{i}(1,1),

then the treatment selection mechanism implies the corresponding monotonicity in potential treatments:

D_{i}(0,0)\leq D_{i}(0,1)\leq D_{i}(1,0)\leq D_{i}(1,1),

where
D_{i}(z,z^{\prime})
denotes the potential treatment under the instrument assignment
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=(z,z^{\prime})
.
This condition ensures that each individual’s treatment increases monotonically with instrument assignments, so that when one unit’s propensity score changes while the other’s remains fixed, we can isolate and identify the corresponding local average controlled spillover or direct effect.

However, when considering the joint treatment vector
(D_{i},D_{-i})
for both group members, the same structure does not guarantee monotonicity at the pairwise level. For example, if
D_{i}(0,1)<D_{i}(1,0)
holds for all units across groups, shifting the instrument assignment from
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=(0,1)
to
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=(1,0)
leads to
D_{i}(1,0)>D_{i}(0,1)
and
D_{-i}(0,1)<D_{-i}(1,0)
. In this case, unit
i
switches from untreated to treated while the peer
-i
switches in the opposite direction, violating monotonicity at the group level.

To restore monotonicity and achieve identification, it is therefore necessary to impose a condition ensuring that at least two of the propensity scores remain constant when the instrument changes. This restriction allows the propensity score of one unit to change while the other’s remains constant, creating variation in treatment decisions where only one unit alters its treatment status. Such variation is essential for identifying the local average controlled spillover and direct effects.

A graphical representation helps clarify this point. Consider the case in which the propensity score values do not satisfy the required equality conditions. When this occurs, local average controlled effects cannot be identified using a binary instrumental variable. Suppose the propensity scores follow a strict ordering,

P_{i}(0,0)<P_{i}(0,1)<P_{i}(1,0)<P_{i}(1,1)

for all units, so that none of the equality conditions hold. In this case, the four observed pairs of propensity scores are represented by the black dots in Figure
5
, which fail to form the “vertices” of a rectangle, illustrating the absence of sufficient variation needed for identification.

Figure 5:
Failure of Point Identification with a Binary Instrument

To illustrate the challenge, consider two pairs of propensity scores,
(P_{i}(0,0),P_{-i}(0,0))
and
(P_{i}(0,1),P_{-i}(1,0))
, and use them to examine identification of the local average controlled spillover effect while holding unit
i
’s treatment fixed at
D_{i}=0
. Following the identification logic developed earlier, the observed conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})D_{-i}\mid\cdot,\cdot]
and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})(1-D_{-i})\mid\cdot,\cdot]
are evaluated at these two pairs of propensity scores.
Taking the difference

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}=P_{i}(0,1),P_{-i}=P_{-i}(1,0)]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}=P_{i}(0,0),P_{-i}=P_{-i}(0,0)],

identifies the average potential outcome
Y_{i}(0,0)
for the subpopulation corresponding to the lighter blue L-shaped region in the left panel of Figure
5
.

Taking another difference.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=P_{i}(0,1),P_{-i}=P_{-i}(1,0)]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=P_{i}(0,0),P_{-i}=P_{-i}(0,0)],

does not identify the average potential outcome
Y_{i}(0,1)
for a well-defined subpopulation, because the regions of
(V_{i},V_{-i})
corresponding to these two treatment realizations, evaluated at the two pairs of propensity scores, partially overlap without one fully containing the other. As illustrated by the two blue-shaded rectangles in the right panel of Figure
5
, this lack of nesting violates the monotonicity condition at the group level.

In this case, it becomes impossible to identify averages of two distinct potential outcomes, such as
Y_{i}(0,0)
and
Y_{i}(0,1)
, for the same subpopulation. Without this, taking their difference to identify local average controlled effects is infeasible. Therefore, the generalized local average controlled spillover and direct effects cannot be point identified unless additional equality conditions on the propensity scores are imposed.

Since propensity scores can be recovered from observed data, these equality conditions can be directly tested. If the required conditions are not satisfied, additional variation in the instrumental variables is necessary to achieve point identification.

Appendix D
Proof of Identification for MCSEs and MCDEs

D.1
Identifying Copula and Copula Density

Under Assumptions
1
-
3
, the propensity score for unit
i
can be shown as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(D_{i}=1\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}\right)

(16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(z_{0},z_{1})\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(z_{0},z_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{i}(z_{0},z_{1}),

given
z_{0}\in\text{Supp}(Z_{i})=\mathbb{R}^{k_{i}},z_{1}\in\text{Supp}(Z_{-i})=\mathbb{R}^{k_{-i}}
. The third equality follows directly from Assumption
1
. Furthermore, under Assumption
3
, we normalize
V_{i}
to follow a uniform distribution
\mathcal{U}(0,1)
, which justifies the final equality. Equation (
16
) shows that the threshold function
h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})
in the treatment mechanism is identified by the propensity score function
P_{i}
on its support
\mathcal{P}_{i}
.

Once the propensity scores
(P_{i},P_{-i})\in\mathcal{P}
of all group members are identified, the copula
C_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})
, which characterizes the dependence structure between the unobserved heterogeneities within the group, can be identified as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid P_{i}\big(Z_{i},Z_{-i}\big)=p_{0},P_{-i}\big(Z_{-i},Z_{i}\big)=p_{1}\big)

(17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i}),V_{-i}\leq h_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})\mid P_{i}\big(Z_{i},Z_{-i}\big)=p_{0},P_{-i}\big(Z_{-i},Z_{i}\big)=p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\mid P_{i}\big(Z_{i},Z_{-i}\big)=p_{0},P_{-i}\big(Z_{-i},Z_{i}\big)=p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})

for
(p_{0},p_{1})\in\mathcal{P}
, where the second equality follows from the identification of the threshold function
h_{i}
by the propensity score
P_{i}
, and the last equality holds under Assumption
1
.

If Assumption
4
holds and
\mathbb{E}[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i},P_{-i}]
is twice differentiable at
(p_{0},p_{1})\in\mathcal{P}
, then the copula density can be identified by taking second-order derivatives,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}

(18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\big)}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}=c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1}).

D.2
Identifying the marginal treatment response functions

Given the values of propensity scores
P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0}
,
P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}
, and any Borel set
A\subset\mathcal{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A\}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\big\{V_{-i}\leq h_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})\big\}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\big\{V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\big\}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\big\{V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\big\}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\big)c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})dv_{0}dv_{1},

where the second equality follows from Equation (
16
), and the third equality holds under Assumption
1
. If the function
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid\cdot,\cdot\big]
is twice differentiable and
m_{i}^{\big(1,1\big)}(\cdot,\cdot)
is continunous at
(p_{0},p_{1})
, by the Leibniz integral rule,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\big]

(19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big)\cdot c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1}).

Since the copula density of
(V_{i},V_{-i})
,
c(\cdot,\cdot)
, is identified from Corollary
1
, we can identify
\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(1,1)\in A\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big)
from Equation (
19
). This, in turn, implies that the marginal treatment response function
m_{i}^{(1,1)}(p_{0},p_{1})
is identified.

We can apply the same procedure and obtain

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A\}D_{i}(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\big]

(20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(1,0)\in A\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big)\cdot c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1}),

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A\}(1-D_{i})D_{-i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(0,1)\in A\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big)\cdot c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1}),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A\}(1-D_{i})(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(0,0)\in A\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big)\cdot c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1}).

We can then identify the remaining marginal treatment response functions from Equation (
20
).

Based on results in Equations (
19
)-(
20
),
\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}^{(d)}(p_{0},p_{1})
and
\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}^{(d)}(p_{0},p_{1})
is identified for any
d\in\{0,1\}
and
p_{0},p_{1}\in\mathcal{P}
.

D.3
Identification With Exogenous Covariates

The identification results can be extended to settings with exogenous covariates. Let
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
denote a vector of covariates that affect both the outcome and the treatment assignment for unit
i
. For instance, unit
i
’s earnings and education choices may depend on the family characteristics of both herself and her best friend in our leading example. Given
(X_{i},X_{-i})=\mathbf{x}
,
D_{i}=d
, and
D_{-i}=d^{\prime}
, we model the potential outcome as

Y_{i}(\mathbf{x},d,d^{\prime})=\mu_{dd^{\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(d,d^{\prime})\big),

(21)

where the functions
\mu_{dd{\prime}}(\cdot,\cdot)
are known and specified by the researcher, while
U_{i}(d,d^{\prime})
captures unobserved factors affecting unit
i
’s potential outcome under own treatment status
D_{i}=d
and the peer
-i
’s treatment
D_{-i}=d^{\prime}
. A common specification for
\mu_{dd^{\prime}}(\cdot,\cdot)
assumes additive separability and linearity in covariates:
\mu_{dd^{\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(d,d^{\prime})\big)=\mathbf{x}\beta_{dd^{\prime}}+U_{i}(d,d^{\prime}).

We next introduce a potential outcome model that incorporates exogenous covariates.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{aligned} Y_{i}=&\big[Y_{i}(X_{i},X_{-i},1,1)D_{-i}+Y_{i}(X_{i},X_{-i},1,0)(1-D_{-i})\big]D_{i}\\
&+\big[Y_{i}(X_{i},X_{-i},0,1)D_{-i}+Y_{i}(X_{i},X_{-i},0,0)(1-D_{-i})\big](1-D_{i}),&\quad\\
D_{i}=&\mathbbm{1}\big\{V_{i}\leq h(W_{i},W_{-i})\big\},\end{aligned}\\
\end{array}\right.

(22)

where
W_{i}\equiv(X_{i},Z_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}\times\mathbb{R}^{k_{i}}
.

Under Equation (
22
), we replace Assumption
1
with Assumption
16
, which imposes random assignment of both covariates and instruments.

Assumption 16
.

(Exogenous covariates and random assignment)
The covariates
X_{i}
and the instruments
Z_{i}
satisfy

\big(X_{i},X_{-i},Z_{i},Z_{-i}\big)\perp\!\!\!\perp\Big\{\big(V_{i},V_{-i},U_{i}(d,d^{\prime}),U_{-i}(d,d^{\prime}),\big)\Big\}_{d\in\{0,1\},d^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}}.

Under Assumptions
2
,
3
, and
16
, the propensity score with exogenous covariates, defined as
P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})\equiv\mathbb{P}(D_{i}=1\mid W_{i},W_{-i})
, can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(D_{i}=1\mid W_{i}=w_{0},W_{-i}=w_{1}\right)

(23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})\mid W_{i}=w_{0},W_{-i}=w_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(w,w^{\prime})\mid W_{i}=w_{0},W_{-i}=w_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(w_{0},w_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{i}(w_{0},w_{1})

given
W_{i}=w_{0},W_{-i}=w_{1}
. Equation (
23
) demonstrates that, in the presence of exogenous covariates, the propensity score
P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})
continues to identify the threshold function
h_{i}
over its support
\mathcal{P}_{i}
.

Similar to Equation (
17
), we can identify the copula function
c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{ig})=p_{1}\big)

(24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq h(W_{i},W_{-i}),V_{-i}\leq h(W_{-i},W_{i})\mid P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{i})=p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\mid P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{i})=p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\right),

where the last equality holds under Assumption
17
. Then, the copula density of
(V_{i},V_{-i})
,
c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(\cdot,\cdot)
, is identifiable provided that the copula
C_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is twice differentiable,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{i})=p_{1}\big)

(25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\big)=c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1}).

The last step is to identify the marginal treatment response functions, defined as
m_{i}^{(\mathbf{x},d,d^{\prime})}(p_{0},p_{1})\equiv\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(\mathbf{x},d,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big]
with covariates. Given the covariates and propensity scores of both units
i
and her peer
-i
, we can express the following conditional expectation as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid(X_{i},X_{-i})=\mathbf{x},P(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P(W_{-i},W_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mu_{11}(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(1,1))\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq h_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{V_{-i}\leq h_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{i})\right\}\mid

\displaystyle(X_{i},X_{-i})=\mathbf{x},P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mu_{11}(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(1,1))\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\right\}\mid

\displaystyle(X_{i},X_{-i})=\mathbf{x},P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{i})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mu_{11}(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(1,1))\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\right\}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mu_{11}(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(1,1))\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\big]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})dv_{0}dv_{1},

where the second equality follows from Equation (
23
), and the third equality holds under Assumption
16
. If the conditional mean
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid(X_{i},X_{-i})=\mathbf{x},\cdot,\cdot]
is twice differentiable, and the marginal treatment response functions
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{dd^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(d,d^{\prime}))\mid\cdot,\cdot]
are continuous at
(p_{0},p_{1})
, then the marginal treatment response (MTR) function
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{11}(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(1,1))\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}]
is identified by taking the cross-derivative as shown below:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid(X_{i},X_{-i})=\mathbf{x},P_{i}(W_{i},W_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(W_{-i},W_{i})=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{11}(\mathbf{x},U_{i}(1,1))\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1}).

Therefore, the MTR function
m_{i}^{(\mathbf{x},1,1)}(p_{0},p_{1})
is identified, given that the copula density is identified as in Equation (
25
). By analogous reasoning, the remaining MTR functions
m_{i}^{(\mathbf{x},d,d^{\prime})}(p_{0},p_{1})
are also identified for all
d,d^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i}}
. The marginal controlled spillover and direct effects are obtained by taking differences between the marginal treatment response (MTR) functions
m_{i}^{(\mathbf{x},d,d^{\prime})}(p_{0},p_{1})
for
d,d^{\prime}\in{0,1}
.

Appendix E
Deriving Policy Relevant Treatment Effects with MCSEs and MCDEs

In this section, we identify the PRTEs under three types of common policy interventions with identified MCSEs and MCDEs.

Case 1: Absolute increase by an exogenous value.
Suppose there exists an alternative policy
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
that exogenously increases the propensity score of all units by a constant
\varepsilon>0
, such that
P^{a^{\prime}}_{i}=P^{a}_{i}+\varepsilon
and
P^{a}_{i},P^{a^{\prime}}_{i}\in[0,1]
, for all
i
in every group. By taking the difference between the expected outcomes under the two policies,
\mathbb{E}[Y^{a}_{i}]
and
\mathbb{E}[Y^{a^{\prime}}_{i}]
, we can express this difference as weighted average of MCDEs and MCSEs as follows,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a}\right]=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\operatorname{MCDE_{i}}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}-\varepsilon\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq p_{0},P_{-i}^{a}\leq p_{1}-\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(P_{i}^{a}\leq p_{0}-\varepsilon,p_{1}-\varepsilon\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}-\varepsilon\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq p_{0},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\right)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}-\varepsilon\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}-\varepsilon\leq P_{i}^{a}<p_{0},p_{1}-\varepsilon\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1}

Once we can identify MCDEs and MCSEs, as well as the joint distributions of propensity scores
(P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a})
and unobservables
(V_{i},V_{-i})
over the full support
[0,1]\times[0,1]
, the policy relevant treatment effect can be point identified as

\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{a^{\prime}}_{i}-Y^{a}_{i}\right]/\Delta P,

where
\Delta P
denotes the proportion of groups in which at least one member changes treatment status as a result of the policy shift from
a
to
a^{\prime}
. This proportion is identified as

\displaystyle\Delta P=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}-\varepsilon\leq P^{a}_{i}\leq p_{0},P_{-i}^{a}\leq p_{1}-\varepsilon\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(P^{a}_{i}\leq p_{0}-\varepsilon,p_{1}-\varepsilon\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}-\varepsilon\leq P^{a}_{i}\leq p_{0},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}\leq P^{a}_{i},p_{1}-\varepsilon\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}-\varepsilon\leq P_{i}^{a}<p_{0},p_{1}-\varepsilon\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1}.

We can also identify the PRTEs for cases where
\varepsilon<0
, or where the policy shift affects group members in opposite directions—for instance,
\varepsilon_{i}>0
and
\varepsilon_{-i}<0
—by applying analogous derivations.

Case 2: Proportional increase by an exogenous value.
Consider an alternative policy
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
that exogenously increases the propensity score of all individuals proportionally, such that
P^{a^{\prime}}_{i}=P^{a}_{i}+\varepsilon(1-P^{a}_{i})
for all individuals
i
, where
0<\varepsilon<1
and
P^{a}_{i},P^{a^{\prime}}_{i}\in[0,1]
. Under this policy shift, we can identify the PRTE as
\mathbb{E}[Y^{a^{\prime}}_{i}-Y^{a}_{i}]/\Delta P
, where

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a}\right]=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\operatorname{MCDE_{i}}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq p_{0},P_{-i}^{a}\leq\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(P_{i}^{a}\leq\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon},\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq p_{0},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\right)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{i}^{a}<p_{0},\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

\displaystyle\Delta P=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq p_{0},P_{-i}^{a}\leq\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left(P_{i}^{a}\leq\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon},\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq p_{0},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{p_{0}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{i}^{a}<p_{0},\frac{p_{1}-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leq P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\right)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1}.

Case 3: Increase the instrument value.
The third type of policy intervention involves shifting the value of certain instruments. For example, consider a policy change where the
j
-th component of the instrument is increased by
\varepsilon
, such that
P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}=P_{i}^{a}(Z+\varepsilon e_{j})
, where
e_{j}
denotes the unit vector in the
j
-th coordinate. In the previous policy changes, the direction of the shift in propensity scores was known for all individuals, allowing us to determine the corresponding changes in treatment responses across the entire range of unobserved characteristics
(V_{i},V_{-i})
. However, when we change the instruments, the effect on propensity scores is not necessarily uniform—some individuals may experience an increase in their propensity scores, while others may see a decrease. The heterogeneous shifts in propensity scores introduce variation in group members’ treatment responses, making the analysis more complicated. To address this problem, we decompose the expected outcome difference,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a}]
, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a}\big]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\}\big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\}\big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\}\big]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\}\big],

with applying the law of total probability. Given that the distribution of
P_{i}^{a}(\cdot)
is identified and
P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}(Z)=P_{i}^{a}(Z+\varepsilon e_{j})
, we can also identify the joint distribution of
(P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a},P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}})
.

We can solve each component in the above equation as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\operatorname{MCDE_{i}}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\big(\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\operatorname{MCDE_{i}}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\big(\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})-\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{-\operatorname{MCDE_{i}}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\big(-\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a})\mathbbm{1}\{P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{-\operatorname{MCDE_{i}}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle-\big(\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}(1;p_{0},p_{1})+\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}(0;p_{0},p_{1})\big)\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

which can be identified once we identify the MCDEs, MCSEs, and the copula density of
(V_{i},V_{-i})
.

Finally, we can identify the PRTE in this case as
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{a^{\prime}}-Y_{i}^{a}]/\Delta P
, where

\displaystyle\Delta P=\Delta P_{1}+\Delta P_{2}+\Delta P_{3}+\Delta P_{4},

\displaystyle\Delta P_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

\displaystyle\Delta P_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

\displaystyle\Delta P_{3}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\geq P_{-i}^{a}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1},

\displaystyle\Delta P_{4}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}>P_{-i}^{a}>P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}>P_{i}^{a}>P_{i}^{a^{\prime}},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{-i}^{a}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(P_{i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{0}\leq P_{i}^{a},P_{-i}^{a^{\prime}}<p_{1}\leq P_{-i}^{a}\big)\bigg\}c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})dp_{0}dp_{1}.

Appendix F
Comparison With Relevant Literature

F.1
Breakdown of MTE causal validity: Proof

We can express
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid p_{i}\left(Z_{i}\right)=p_{0}\right]
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i})=p_{1}\right]\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}\right]

by applying the law of iterated expectations. The inner conditional expectation can be further expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i})=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}(1-D_{-i})\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i})=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})\}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{-i}\leq h_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})\}\mid h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},h_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})\}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{-i}>h_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})\}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{-i}>p_{1}\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})dv_{0}dv_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})dv_{0}dv_{1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{0}dv_{1}dp_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{0}dv_{1}dp_{1},

where
f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(\cdot)
denotes the conditional density of propensity score function
P_{-i}
given
P_{i}=p_{0}
. If
Y_{i}
is bounded, i.e.,
|Y_{i}|<\infty
, then by Fubini’s theorem, we can interchange the order of integration in the expression above, yielding the following result.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0}.

Suppose the above function is continuously differentiable with respect to
p_{0}
. In that case, we can apply the Leibniz integral rule to differentiate and obtain the following equalities,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{11},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{10}.

The terms
\mathcal{R}_{11}
and
\mathcal{R}_{10}
are nonzero because
c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})\neq 0
, and
\partial f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})/\partial p_{0}\neq 0
given that
P_{i}
and
P_{-i}
are dependent as they are both functions of
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})
.

Similarly, under the assumption that
Y_{i}
is bounded and that
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}\right]
is continuously differentiable with respect to
p_{0}
, we obtain the following equalities,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(0,1)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{01}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(0,0)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}+\mathcal{R}_{00},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{01}=\int_{p_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(0,1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{00}=\int_{p_{0}}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(0,0)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}dv_{0}.

By taking the difference between
\partial\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}D_{i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}]/\partial p_{0}
and
-\partial\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(1-D_{i})\mid P_{i}(Z_{i})=p_{0}]/\partial p_{0}
, we would identify

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,1)-Y_{i}(0,1)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{p_{1}}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,0)-Y_{i}(0,0)\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\right]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},v_{1})f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})dv_{1}dp_{1}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{R},

where the bias term
\mathcal{R}
is given by the sum of its components
\mathcal{R}_{dd^{\prime}}
, where
d,d^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
, as defined in the preceding derivations.

The first two terms correspond to the averages of the marginal controlled direct effects for unit
i
,
\operatorname{MCDE}i^{(1)}(p_{0},v_{1})
and
\operatorname{MCDE}i^{(0)}(p_{0},v_{1})
, weighted by the peer’s propensity scores conditional on
P_{i}=p_{0}
and the copula density of
(V_{i},V{-i})
. Since the bias terms,
\mathcal{R}
, involves the derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1}),

it vanishes whenever this derivative equals zero for all
p_{1}
, i.e., when
f_{P_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0}}(p_{1})
does not depend on
p_{0}
. This holds when the propensity scores
P_{i}
and
P_{-i}
are independent, which occurs under two sufficient conditions:

1.

The instruments
Z_{i}
and
Z_{-i}
are independent within each group.

2.

The threshold function for unit
i
depends solely on its own instrument,
h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=h_{i}(Z_{i})
.

Under these conditions, since the propensity score identifies the threshold function, it follows that
P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=h_{i}(Z_{i})
and
P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i})=h_{-i}(Z_{-i})
almost surely. Since
Z_{i}
is assumed to be independent of
Z_{-i}
,
P_{i}
and
P_{-i}
are independent, implying that the bias term
\mathcal{R}
equals zero.

F.2
Marginal Controlled Effects in the Absence of Spillovers

If spillover effects are absent in both income and treatment selection, which means that
V_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp V_{-i}
,
Y_{i}(D_{i},d)=Y_{i}(D_{i},d^{\prime})\equiv Y_{i}(D_{i})
, and
h_{i}(Z_{i},z)=h_{i}(Z_{i},z^{\prime})\equiv h_{i}(Z_{i})
, then our identification results reduce to the standard MTE framework.

In this case, the propensity score identifies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(z_{0})\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq h_{i}(z_{0})\big)=h_{i}(z_{0}),

which is the threshold function from the standard MTE setting, where peer instruments do not influence individual treatment decisions.

Taking the cross-partial derivative of
\mathbb{P}(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1})
, the copula density evaluated at the point
(p_{0},p_{1})
,
c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})
, is identified as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq p_{0},V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}\big(V_{i}\leq p_{0}\big)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{P}\big(V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\big)=1.

The copula density equals one since
V_{i}
and
V_{-i}
are independent. This aligns with the standard MTE framework, where the individuals’ unobserved heterogeneities are independent.

Finally, by taking the cross-partial derivative of
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p_{0}\}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{-i}\leq p_{1}\}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1}\big]c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})dv_{0}dv_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{1}}\int_{0}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1)\mid V_{i}=v_{0}\big]dv_{0}dv_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1)\mid V_{i}=p_{0}\big],

where the third line follows from Assumption
1
, the fifth line holds because
V_{-i}
is independent with
(Y_{i}(d),V_{i})
, and the copula density equals to one. By analogous reasoning, taking the cross-partial derivatives of
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d,D_{i}=d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
identifies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d,D_{i}=d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(d)\mid V_{i}=p_{0}\big],

and the cross-partial derivatives of
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d,D_{-i}=1-d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}]
identifies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d,D_{-i}=1-d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(d)\mid V_{i}=p_{0}\big],

for
d\in\{0,1\}
and
(p_{0},p_{1})
being an interior point of
\mathcal{P}
. Then, it follows that the
\operatorname{MCSE}_{i}^{(d)}(p_{0},p_{1})
is identified as

\operatorname{sgn}(2d-1)\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\right]}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}=0,

and the
\operatorname{MCDE}_{i}^{(d)}(p_{0},p_{1})
is identified as

\operatorname{sgn}(2d-1)\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{-i}=d\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\right]}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}=\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}(1)-Y_{i}(0)\mid V_{i}=p_{0}\big].

One may interpret the model in Equation (
1
) as a standard MTE framework, treating
(D_{-i},Z_{-i})
as covariates. This raises a natural question: can the marginal treatment response (MTR) functions be identified using conventional MTE methods by conditioning on the peer’s treatment status
D_{-i}
? The answer is affirmative, provided that
D_{-i}
is exogenous with respect to unit
i
’s potential outcomes and unobserved heterogeneity. Specifically, this requires the independence assumption

(Z_{i},Z_{-i},D_{-i})\perp\!\!\!\perp\{(V_{i},Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime}))\}_{d\in\{0,1\},d^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}}.

(26)

When this condition holds, the marginal treatment response functions conditional on the individual’s own unobserved heterogeneity,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}]
, can be identified using the standard MTE identification strategy, extended to include
D_{-i}
as an additional covariate. For instance,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\cdot D_{i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p,D_{-i}=d^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p\}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p,D_{-i}=d^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\int_{0}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}=v\right]dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}=p\right].

It is important to note that the second equality in the above derivation holds only if
D_{-i}
satisfies the independence condition in Equation (
26
).

However, the assumption of exogeneity for
D_{-i}
is not realistic within our spillover framework. As illustrated in Figure
1
, the peer’s unobserved heterogeneity
V_{-i}
affects her treatment
D_{-i}
and may also be correlated with unit
i
’s unobserved confounder
V_{i}
, thereby threatening the exogeneity of
D_{-i}
. In the marginal spillover setting, where
V_{i}
and
V_{-i}
are allowed to be arbitrarily correlated, applying the standard MTE identification strategy by conditioning on
D_{-i}
would lead to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}\cdot D_{i}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p,D_{-i}=d^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{V_{i}\leq p\}\mid P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i})=p,D_{-i}=d^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\int_{0}^{p}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}=v,D_{-i}=1\right]f_{V_{i}\mid D_{-i}=d}(v)dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}=p,D_{-i}=d^{\prime}\right],

with the distribution of
V_{i}\mid D_{-i}=d
being normalized to follow a uniform distribution on
[0,1]
. However,
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}=p,D_{-i}=d^{\prime}\right]
is not equal to
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{i}(1,d^{\prime})\mid V_{i}=p\right]
, because
D_{-i}
is determined by
V_{-i}
, which is dependent on
Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime})
even conditioning on
V_{i}
, as illustrated in Figure
1
.

F.3
Comparing With Multivalued Treatments Literature

In this section, we compare the methods for identifying marginal spillover effects with the framework discussed in
27
. We focus on the group level and consider the treatment vector
\boldsymbol{D}_{g}\equiv(D_{0g},D_{1g})
, assigned to each group
g
. The treatment vector
\boldsymbol{D}_{g}
takes values from the set
\{(1,1),(1,0),(0,1),(0,0)\}
, consisting of four elements. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{D}_{g}
can be regarded as multivalued treatments assigned at the group level. To align with the notation in
27
, we relabel the treatment vectors as follows:
(0,0)\equiv 0
,
(0,1)\equiv 1
,
(1,0)\equiv 2
, and
(1,1)\equiv 3
. Consequently, the treatment
\boldsymbol{D}_{g}
takes values from
\{0,1,2,3\}\equiv\mathcal{D}
.

Each group is randomly assigned a continuous instrumental variable, with the instrument vector for group
g
denoted as
\boldsymbol{Z}_{g}\equiv(Z_{0g},Z_{1g})
. Let
V_{ig}\in\mathbb{R}
represent the unobserved characteristics of individual
i
in group
g
, and let
\boldsymbol{V}_{g}\equiv(V_{0g},V_{1g})
denote the vector of unobserved heterogeneity for both individuals in group
g
. For simplicity, we omit the group subscript
g
from the notation. The parameters of interest in
27
,
E(Y_{k}\mid\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{v})-E(Y_{k^{\prime}}\mid\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{v})
, where
k\neq k^{\prime}
and
k,k^{\prime}\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, can be interpreted as marginal controlled spillover effects and marginal controlled direct effects within the spillover framework.

According to the model in Equation (
1
), we have

1.

\boldsymbol{D}=0
if and only if
V_{0}>h_{0}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
V_{1}>h_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
.

2.

\boldsymbol{D}=1
if and only if
V_{0}>h_{0}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
V_{1}\leq h_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
.

3.

\boldsymbol{D}=2
if and only if
V_{0}\leq h_{0}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
V_{1}>h_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
.

4.

\boldsymbol{D}=3
if and only if
V_{0}\leq h_{0}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
V_{1}\leq h_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
.

It is straightforward to see that the treatment
\boldsymbol{D}
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-field generated by the events
\left\{V_{i}<Q_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})\right\}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
, which aligns with the selection mechanism described in Assumption 2.1 of
27
. Furthermore, Theorem 3.1 in
26
is similar to our approach in identifying the joint density of unobserved heterogeneity
\boldsymbol{V}
and the marginal treatment response functions, once the threshold functions
h_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
(denoted as
Q_{i}(\boldsymbol{Z})
in
27
) are identified.

However, in our setting, fewer assumptions are needed to point identify the thresholds. Specifically, we only require that the instruments
\boldsymbol{Z}
are randomly assigned at the group level and do not directly influence the outcomes, without relying on the additional exclusion restrictions on instruments imposed in Assumption 4.1 of
27
. This is because we have more information on the observed treatments
D
, allowing us to identify the marginal distributions of
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
from the proportions of observed treatments:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{D}=3\mid\boldsymbol{Z}=z)+\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{D}=2\mid\boldsymbol{Z}=z)=\mathbb{P}(V_{0}\leq h_{0}(z)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{D}=3\mid\boldsymbol{Z}=z)+\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{D}=1\mid\boldsymbol{Z}=z)=\mathbb{P}(V_{1}\leq h_{1}(z)).

Remark 8
.

(Monotonicity for each unit) When we focus on each individual unit
i
within a group, the monotonicity condition is satisfied. Specifically, consider any two vectors of instruments, denoted as
(z_{0},z_{1})
and
(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})
, where
P_{i}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq P_{i}(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})
. Under the monotonicity assumption, this ordering of the propensity scores implies that the corresponding potential treatments satisfy
D_{i}(z_{0},z_{1})\leq D_{i}(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})
. However, if we treat the entire group as a single decision-making unit and reformulate the setting into a multivalued treatment model, the monotonicity assumption may no longer hold. For instance, in the two-way flow model discussed in
27
, when the proportion of
D=2
changes, it is unclear whether the shift is driven by changes in
h_{0}
or
h_{1}
. In other words, shifts in either
h_{0}
or
h_{1}
can induce changes in the proportion of receiving a given treatment, making it impossible to distinguish between the two effects. As a result, the monotonicity condition is violated, and the marginal distributions of
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
cannot be identified.

In the multivalued treatment setting discussed in
27
, if we have enough information on the observed treatment
D
that allows us to identify the threshold
h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,J\}
, then we can point identify the joint density of
\boldsymbol{V}
and the marginal treatment response functions in Theorem 3.1 of
27
. Specifically, for each dimension
j\in\{1,\cdots,J\}
of the unobservable
\boldsymbol{V}
, we need a subset of the support of the treatments,
\mathcal{K}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{K}=\{0,\cdots,K-1\}
, such that

\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(D=k\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(V_{j}\leq h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(V_{j}\leq h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})\right)=h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z}).

If
\mathcal{K}_{j}
that satisfies the above conditions does not exist for some
j
, we can still partially identify the threshold
h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
.
h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})
can be partially identified as

\sum_{k\in\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(D=k\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(V_{j}\leq h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(V_{j}\leq h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})\right)=h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})\leq\sum_{k\in\bar{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}\mathbb{P}\left(D=k\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right),

where
\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
is the largest subset
\widetilde{\underline{\mathcal{K}}}_{j}
of
\mathcal{K}
such that

\cup_{k\in\widetilde{\underline{\mathcal{K}}}_{j}}d_{k}^{-1}\{D=k\}\subseteq\{V_{j}\leq h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})\},

and
\bar{\mathcal{K}_{j}}
is the smallest subset
\widetilde{\bar{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}
of
\mathcal{K}
such that

\{V_{j}\leq h_{j}(\boldsymbol{Z})\}\subseteq\cup_{k\in\widetilde{\bar{\mathcal{K}_{j}}}}d_{k}^{-1}\{D=k\}.

For example, in the two-way flow model discussed in
27
, we can partially identify
h_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})
and
h_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(D=0\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)\leq h_{1}(\boldsymbol{Z})\leq\mathbb{P}\left(D=0\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(D=2\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(D=0\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)\leq h_{2}(\boldsymbol{Z})\leq\mathbb{P}\left(D=0\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(D=2\mid\boldsymbol{Z}\right).

Appendix G
Derivations of Testable Implications

Corollary
1
