establishes identification of the copula density. Because any valid copula density must be nonnegative, this result directly implies a testable inequality restriction:

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{i}D_{-i}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\big]}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\geq 0

if the cross-derivative correctly identifies the copula density. This constitutes one of the testable implications.

In addition, the proof of Theorem
2
identifies the conditional joint distributions of potential outcomes,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime})\in A_{1},Y_{-i}(d,d^{\prime})\in A_{2}\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1})
, weighted by the copula density
c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})
. By the nonnegativity of both probabilities and copula densities, this quantity must be nonnegative. Together with the inequality derived from the copula density, this yields a set of testable inequality restrictions that must hold for any Borel sets
A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
and all
d\in\{0,1\}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}\big\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\big\}\mathbbm{1}\big\{D_{i}=d,D_{-i}=d\big\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(d,d)\in A_{1},Y_{-i}(d,d)\in A_{2}\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big)c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})\geq 0,

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{1}\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}\big\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\big\}\mathbbm{1}\big\{D_{i}=d,D_{-i}=1-d\big\}\mid P_{i}=p_{0},P_{-i}=p_{1}\Big]\geq 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{i}(d,1-d)\in A_{1},Y_{-i}(d,1-d)\in A_{2}\mid V_{i}=p_{0},V_{-i}=p_{1}\big)c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})\geq 0.

It is worth noting that both the copula function and the marginal treatment response functions are functions of the propensity scores rather than the instrument values themselves. This feature implies an additional set of testable implications. In particular, if two distinct pairs of instrument values,
(z_{0},z_{1})\neq(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})
, yield the same propensity scores,
P_{i}(z_{0},z_{1})=P_{i}(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})=p_{0}
and
P_{-i}(z_{0},z_{1})=P_{-i}(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})=p_{1}
, then any identified quantities that depend only on
(p_{0},p_{1})
, such as the copula density or the marginal treatment response functions, must be equal across these instrument values. This leads to the following testable equality

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid P_{i}(z_{0},z_{1})=p_{0},P_{-i}(z_{1},z_{0})=p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid P_{i}(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})=p_{0},P_{-i}(\tilde{z}_{1},\tilde{z}_{0})=p_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid Z_{i}=\tilde{z}_{0},Z_{-i}=\tilde{z}_{1}\big),

since both conditional probabilities
\mathbb{P}(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1})
and
\mathbb{P}(D_{i}=1,D_{-i}=1\mid Z_{i}=\tilde{z}_{0},Z_{-i}=\tilde{z}_{1})
identify the copula function
C_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(p_{0},p_{1})
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d_{0},D_{-i}=d_{1}\}\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d_{0},D_{-i}=d_{1}\}\mid P_{i}(z_{0},z_{1})=p_{0},P_{-i}(z_{1},z_{0})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d_{0},D_{-i}=d_{1}\}\mid P_{i}(\tilde{z}_{0},\tilde{z}_{1})=p_{0},P_{-i}(\tilde{z}_{1},\tilde{z}_{0})=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d_{0},D_{-i}=d_{1}\}\mid Z_{i}=\tilde{z}_{0},Z_{-i}=\tilde{z}_{1}\big],

since both conditional means
\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d_{0},D_{-i}=d_{1}\}\mid Z_{i}=z_{0},Z_{-i}=z_{1}]
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}\{Y_{i}\in A_{1},Y_{-i}\in A_{2}\}\mathbbm{1}\{D_{i}=d_{0},D_{-i}=d_{1}\}\mid Z_{i}=\tilde{z}_{0},Z_{-i}=\tilde{z}_{1}]
identify the same integral of
\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}(d_{0},d_{1})\in A_{1},Y_{-i}(d_{0},d_{1})\in A_{2}\mid V_{i}=v_{0},V_{-i}=v_{1})
weighted by the copula density
c_{V_{i},V_{-i}}(v_{0},v_{1})
, according to the proof of Theorem
2
.

To conclude, these insights yield the sets of testable implications stated in Corollary
4
.

Appendix H
Proof of asymptotic results

H.1
Convergence rate of nonparametric cross-derivative estimators

To simplify the notation, we introduce the following matrix definitions,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\equiv\left[\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1&\big(\hat{P}_{01}-p_{0}\big)&\cdots&\big(\hat{P}_{01}-p_{0}\big)\big(\hat{P}_{11}-p_{1}\big)&\cdots&\big(\hat{P}_{11}-p_{1}\big)^{3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1&\big(\hat{P}_{0G}-p_{0}\big)&\cdots&\big(\hat{P}_{0G}-p_{0}\big)\big(\hat{P}_{1G}-p_{1}\big)&\cdots&\big(\hat{P}_{1G}-p_{1}\big)^{3},\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h}\equiv\operatorname{diag}\Big(K_{h}\big(\hat{P}_{1}-p\big),\cdots,K_{h}\big(\hat{P}_{G}-p\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\equiv\big[D_{01}D_{11},\cdots,D_{0G}D_{1G}\big]^{\prime},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}}=\big[\hat{U}_{idd^{\prime}1},\cdots,\hat{U}_{idd^{\prime}G}\big]^{\prime},

where
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}
is a
G\times 10
matrix of regressors used in the local polynomial regression,
\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h}
is a
G\times G
diagonal matrix consisting of the kernel functions,
\mathbf{D}
is a
G\times 1
vector entries
D_{0g}D_{1g}
, and
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}}
is a
G\times 1
vector residuals
\hat{U}_{idd^{\prime}g}
as entries. Using this notation, we can express the estimators
\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})
and
\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})
derived in Section
3.1.1
as

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})=e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{D},

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})=e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}},

where
e_{5}
is the
10\times 1
standard basis vector with a one in the fifth entry and zeros elsewhere.

We aim to characterize the asymptotic behavior of

e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{D}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big].

(27)

We rewrite Equation (
27
) as

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{D}^{*}\big]+e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{D}^{*}

\displaystyle-e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\mathbf{D}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{D}^{*}\big]+e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{D}^{*}-\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\mathbf{D}_{*}\big],

and define
\mathbf{D}^{*},\mathbf{D}_{*}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{*}\equiv

\displaystyle\big[\mathbb{E}[D_{01}D_{11}\mid P_{01},P_{11}],\cdots,\mathbb{E}[D_{0G}D_{1G}\mid P_{0G},P_{1G}]\big]^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{*}\equiv

\displaystyle\Big[\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big],\cdots,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big],

\displaystyle\cdots,\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial p_{1}^{3}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]\Big]^{\prime},

where
\mathbf{D}^{*}
is a
G\times 1
vector consisting of the conditional means
\mathbb{E}[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g},P_{1g}],g=1,\cdots,G
, and
\mathbf{D}_{*}
is a
10\times 1
vector comprising the partial derivatives of
\mathbb{E}[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}]
up to third order.

We apply Taylor series expansion to expand the conditional mean
\mathbb{E}[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g},P_{1g}]
around
(p_{0},p_{1})
and express it as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]+\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big](P_{0g}-p_{0})

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big](P_{1g}-p_{1})+\cdots

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial^{3}}{6\partial p_{1}^{3}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big](P_{1g}-p_{1})^{3}+R_{P}(p_{0},p_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]+\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]\big[(P_{0g}-\hat{P}_{0g})+(\hat{P}_{0g}-p_{0})\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]\big[(P_{1g}-\hat{P}_{1g})+(\hat{P}_{1g}-p_{1})\big]+\cdots

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial^{3}}{6\partial p_{1}^{3}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]\big[(P_{1g}-\hat{P}_{1g})+(\hat{P}_{1g}-p_{1})\big]^{3}+R_{P}(p_{0},p_{1})

where
R_{P}(p_{0},p_{1})
represents the remainder terms from the Taylor expansion, and the last step is to decompose
(P_{ig}-p_{i})
as
[(P_{ig}-\hat{P}_{ig})+(\hat{P}_{ig}-p_{i})]
,
i\in\{0,1\}
. Noting that the
g
th entry of
[\mathbf{D}^{*}-\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\mathbf{D}_{*}]
equals to

\displaystyle\big[\mathbf{D}^{*}-\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\mathbf{D}_{*}\big]_{g}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g},P_{1g}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big](\hat{P}_{0g}-p_{0})-\cdots

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial p_{1}^{3}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big](\hat{P}_{1g}-p_{1})^{3},

we can leverage the expansion results to show

\displaystyle\big[\mathbf{D}^{*}-\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\mathbf{D}_{*}\big]_{g}=O_{P}\Big[\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{0g}-P_{0g}|+\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{1g}-P_{1g}|+h_{G1}^{4}\Big].

The results in Section
3.1
, combined with the boundedness of the kernel assumed in Assumption
9
, imply that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}-\mathbf{X}_{P}\big\|\ =o_{P}(1),\big\|\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}-\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}\big\|=o_{P}(1),

where we define
\mathbf{X}_{P}
and
\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{P}\equiv\left[\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1&\big(P_{01}-p_{0}\big)&\cdots&\big(P_{01}-p_{0}\big)\big(P_{11}-p_{1}\big)&\cdots&\big(P_{11}-p_{1}\big)^{3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1&\big(P_{0G}-p_{0}\big)&\cdots&\big(P_{0G}-p_{0}\big)\big(P_{1G}-p_{1}\big)&\cdots&\big(P_{1G}-p_{1}\big)^{3},\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{h}\equiv\operatorname{diag}\Big(K_{h}\big(P_{1}-p\big),\cdots,K_{h}\big(P_{G}-p\big)\Big).

Since
\big(\mathbf{X}_{P}^{\prime}\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{X}_{P}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{P}^{\prime}\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}=O_{P}(1)
by assumptions, it follows that

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}=O_{P}(1)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle e_{5}^{\prime}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{D}^{*}-\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\mathbf{D}_{*}\big]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\Big[\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{0g}-P_{0g}|+\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{1g}-P_{1g}|+h_{G1}^{4}\Big].

Additionally,

e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{D}^{*}\big]=e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\mathbf{X}_{P}^{\prime}\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{X}_{P}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{P}^{\prime}\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{D}^{*}\big]\big[1+o_{P}(1)\big].

By applying the results from
30
, the term
e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\mathbf{X}_{P}^{\prime}\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{X}_{P}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{P}^{\prime}\mathbf{W}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{D}^{*}\big]
converges at the rate
O_{P}[(Gh_{G1}^{6})^{-1/2}]
. the convergence rate of Equation (
27
) is given by

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{D}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{P}\Big[(Gh_{G1}^{6})^{-1/2}+\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{0g}-P_{0g}|+\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{1g}-P_{1g}|+h_{G1}^{4}\Big]

Furthermore, we can show that

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[U_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\big[\mathbf{U}_{idd^{\prime}}-X_{i}(\hat{\beta}_{dd^{\prime}}-\beta_{dd^{\prime}})\big]

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[U_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\mathbf{U}_{idd^{\prime}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[U_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]+O_{P}(G^{-1/2}),

where the last equation holds by
\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}=O_{P}(1)
,
\hat{\beta}_{dd^{\prime}}-\beta_{dd^{\prime}}=O_{P}(G^{-1/2})
according to Theorem 3 in
13
, and
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}},\mathbf{U}_{idd^{\prime}}
are defined as

\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}}=\big[\hat{U}_{idd^{\prime}1},\cdots,\hat{U}_{idd^{\prime}G}\big]^{\prime},\mathbf{U}_{idd^{\prime}}=\big[U_{idd^{\prime}1},\cdots,U_{idd^{\prime}G}\big]^{\prime}.

Then, the convergence rate of
e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}}
can be proven as

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{5}\big(\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}_{P}\big)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{\prime}_{P}\widehat{\mathbf{W}}_{h_{G1}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{idd^{\prime}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[U_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O_{P}\Big[(Gh_{G2}^{6})^{-1/2}+\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{0g}-P_{0g}|+\max_{g:1\leq g\leq G}|\hat{P}_{1g}-P_{1g}|+h_{G2}^{4}\Big]

using an argument analogous to that used in the preceding analysis.

H.2
Asymptotic distribution of nonparametric marginal treatment response estimators

Under Assumption
10
, combined with conclusions in
30
, we have

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Big\{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})-c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})\Big\}\xrightarrow{d}N\Big(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{f(p_{0},p_{1})}\big(M^{-1}\Gamma M^{-1}\big)_{5,5}\Big),

\displaystyle c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})\equiv\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}}\mathbb{E}\big[U_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}\big],

where
\sigma^{2}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})=\text{Var}(U_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1})
, and
f(p_{0},p_{1})
denotes the density of
(P_{0g},P_{1g})
evaluated at the point
(p_{0},p_{1})
. The matrices
M
and
\Gamma
are
10\times 10
-matrices composed of multivariate moments of the kernel functions
K
and
K^{2}
, and are defined as

\displaystyle M=\left[\begin{array}[]{lllll}\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{0}K(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{0}K(u)d(u)&\cdots&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{2}K(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{3}K(u)d(u)\\
\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{0}K(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{2}u_{1}^{0}K(u)d(u)&\cdots&\int u_{0}^{2}u_{1}^{2}K(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{3}K(u)d(u)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{3}K(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{2}K(u)d(u)&\cdots&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{5}K(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{6}K(u)d(u)\\
\end{array}\right],

\displaystyle\Gamma=\left[\begin{array}[]{lllll}\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{0}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{0}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\cdots&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{2}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{3}K^{2}(u)d(u)\\
\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{0}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{2}u_{1}^{0}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\cdots&\int u_{0}^{2}u_{1}^{2}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{3}K^{2}(u)d(u)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{3}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{2}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\cdots&\int u_{0}^{1}u_{1}^{5}K^{2}(u)d(u)&\int u_{0}^{0}u_{1}^{6}K^{2}(u)d(u)\\
\end{array}\right].

Since
\partial^{2}\mathbb{E}[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}]/\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}
is bounded from above and away from zero by Assumption
9
and
\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})\xrightarrow{p}\partial^{2}\mathbb{E}[D_{0g}D_{1g}\mid P_{0g}=p_{0},P_{1g}=p_{1}]/\partial p_{0}\partial p_{1}\equiv b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})-c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})\Bigg\}\frac{1}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}

\displaystyle N\Bigg(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\big(b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})\big)^{2}f(p_{0},p_{1})}\big(M^{-1}\Gamma M^{-1}\big)_{5,5}\Bigg)

We can rewrite
\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Big\{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})-c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})\Big\}\big/\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})
as

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})-c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})\Bigg\}\frac{1}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}+\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Bigg\}.

By applying Assumption
10
along with the results in Section
3.1
, we obtain

\frac{1}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{1}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}=O_{P}\big[(Gh_{G1}^{6})^{-1/2}\big],

which implies that

\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Bigg\}=o_{P}(1)

under the condition
h_{G2}=o(h_{G1})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})-c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})\Bigg\}\frac{1}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}+o_{P}(1),

and the asymptotic distribution of estimated marginal treatment response function without the covariate effect can be characterized as

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}

\displaystyle N\Bigg(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})}{\big(b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})\big)^{2}f(p_{0},p_{1})}\big(M^{-1}\Gamma M^{-1}\big)_{5,5}\Bigg).

Finally, under the assumptions that
\hat{c}_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})/\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})-c_{4}(d,d^{\prime};p_{0},p_{1})/b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})
are asymptotically independent across different values of
d,d^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
, we can derive the asymptotic distributions of
\widehat{\text{MCSE}}(\mathbf{x},d;p_{0},p_{1})
as

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\widehat{\text{MCSE}}(\mathbf{x},d;p_{0},p_{1})-\text{MCSE}(\mathbf{x},d;p_{0},p_{1})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Big[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\big(\hat{\beta}_{d1}-\beta_{d1}\big)+\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\big(\hat{\beta}_{d0}-\beta_{d0}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle+\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,1;p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,1;p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Big)+\Big(\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,0;p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,0;p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{P}(1)+\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,1;p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,1;p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Big)+\Big(\frac{\hat{c}_{4}(d,0;p_{0},p_{1})}{\hat{b}_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}-\frac{c_{4}(d,0;p_{0},p_{1})}{b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})}\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}N\Bigg(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}(d,1;p_{0},p_{1})+\sigma^{2}(d,0;p_{0},p_{1})}{\big(b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})\big)^{2}f(p_{0},p_{1})}\big(M^{-1}\Gamma M^{-1}\big)_{5,5}\Bigg),

where the second equality holds because
\hat{\beta}_{dd^{\prime}}-\beta_{dd^{\prime}}=O_{P}(G^{-1/2})
applying Theorem 3 in
13
. Similarly, the asymptotic distribution of
\widehat{\text{MCDE}}(\mathbf{x},d;p_{0},p_{1})
can be derived as

\displaystyle\big(Gh_{G2}^{6}\big)^{1/2}\Bigg\{\widehat{\text{MCSE}}(\mathbf{x},d;p_{0},p_{1})-\text{MCSE}(\mathbf{x},d;p_{0},p_{1})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}N\Bigg(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}(1,d;p_{0},p_{1})+\sigma^{2}(0,d;p_{0},p_{1})}{\big(b_{4}(p_{0},p_{1})\big)^{2}f(p_{0},p_{1})}\big(M^{-1}\Gamma M^{-1}\big)_{5,5}\Bigg).

H.3
Consistency of the parametric first-stage estimator

For each
i\in\{0,1\}
, the function
l(\theta_{i};d,\mathbf{w})
is continuous in
\theta_{i}
for all
d\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{W}
. Additionally, the parameter space
\Theta_{i}
is compact and
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}|l(\theta_{i};D_{ig},W_{g})|]<\infty
given Assumption
12
, applying the uniform law of large numbers, we have

\sup_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\Bigg|\frac{1}{G}\sum_{g=1}^{G}l(\theta_{i};D_{ig},W_{g})-\mathbb{E}\big[l(\theta_{i};D_{ig},W_{g})\big]\Bigg|\overset{a.s.}{\to}0,

as
G\rightarrow\infty
. Define
Q(\theta_{i})=\mathbb{E}\big[l(\theta_{i};D_{ig},W_{g})\big]
and
Q_{G}(\theta_{i})=\sum_{g=1}^{G}l(\theta_{i};D_{ig},W_{g})/G
. We can derive

\displaystyle 0\leq Q(\theta_{i0})-Q(\hat{\theta}_{i})

\displaystyle=Q_{G}(\hat{\theta}_{i})-Q(\hat{\theta}_{i})+Q(\theta_{i0})-Q_{G}(\hat{\theta}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\big|Q_{G}(\theta_{i})-Q(\theta_{i})\big|+Q(\theta_{i0})-Q_{G}(\theta_{i0})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{\theta_{i}\in\Theta_{i}}\left|Q_{G}(\theta_{i})-Q(\theta_{i})\right|

\displaystyle\overset{a.s.}{\to}0,

as
G\rightarrow\infty
, where the second line holds because
\hat{\theta}_{i}
maximizes the function
Q_{G}(\theta_{i})
. Since
\theta_{i}
is the unique maximizer of
Q(\theta_{i})
and
\Theta_{i}
is compact based on Assumption
12
,
Q_{G}(\hat{\theta}_{i})\overset{a.s.}{\to}Q(\theta_{i0})
implies that
\hat{\theta}_{i}\overset{a.s.}{\to}\theta_{i0}
as
G\rightarrow\infty
.

H.4
Consistency of the parametric second-stage estimator

We use
Q_{G}(\rho)
and
\widehat{Q}_{G}(\rho)
to define

\displaystyle Q_{G}(\rho)=\frac{1}{G}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\tilde{l}\big(\rho;D_{g},P_{g}\big),

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{G}(\rho)=\frac{1}{G}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\tilde{l}\big(\rho;D_{g},\widehat{P}_{g}\big),

where
\widehat{P}_{g}=(\widehat{P}_{0g},\widehat{P}_{1g})
is the vector of propensity scores estimated in the first stage. Then, we can write

\displaystyle\bigg|\widehat{Q}_{G}(\rho)-\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},P_{g})\big]\bigg|\leq

\displaystyle\bigg|\widehat{Q}_{G}(\rho)-Q_{G}(\rho)\bigg|

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\bigg|Q_{G}(\rho)-\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},P_{g})\big]\bigg|.

Since
\tilde{l}(\rho;d,p)
is continuous in
\rho
for all
d\in\{0,1\}^{2}
and
p\in(0,1)^{2}
,
\rho
lies in a compact interval, and
\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{\rho\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]}|l(\rho,\theta;D_{g},W_{g})|\big]<\infty
under Assumption
13
, the law of large numbers implies that

\sup_{\rho\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]}\bigg|Q_{G}(\rho)-\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},P_{g})\big]\bigg|\overset{a.s.}{\to}0.

Assuption
13
also assumes that there exists a function
L(\cdot)
such that for all
\rho\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
,

\big|\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},\widehat{P}_{g})-\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},P_{g})\big|\leq L(D_{g})\big|\big|\widehat{P}_{g}-P_{g}\big|\big|.

Since
|L(D_{g})|<\infty
almost surely and
\big|\big|\widehat{P}_{g}-P_{g}\big|\big|\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
by Lemma
4
, it follows that
\sup_{\rho\in[\varepsilon,\varepsilon]}\big|\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},\widehat{P}_{g})-\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},P_{g})\big|\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
, which further implies

\sup_{\rho\in[\varepsilon,\varepsilon]}\bigg|\widehat{Q}_{G}(\rho)-Q_{G}(\rho)\bigg|\overset{a.s.}{\to}0.

Therefore, we have

\sup_{\rho\in[\varepsilon,\varepsilon]}\bigg|\widehat{Q}_{G}(\rho)-\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},P_{g})\big]\bigg|\overset{a.s.}{\to}0.

Since
\rho_{0}
is the unique maximizer of
\mathbb{E}\big[\tilde{l}(\rho;D_{g},P_{g})\big]
and lies within a compact interval, by the similar arguments in the proof of Lemma
4
, we can derive
\hat{\rho}\overset{a.s.}{\to}\rho_{0}
as
G\rightarrow\infty
.

H.5
Consistency of the parametric MTR coefficient estimates

Based on the identification results the third specification in Assumption
11
, for each
i\in\{0,1\}
and
g\in\{1,\cdots,G\}
,

Y_{ig}\mathbbm{1}\big\{D_{0g}=d\big\}\mathbbm{1}\big\{D_{1g}=d^{\prime}\big\}=X_{Pdd^{\prime}_{g}}\big(\alpha^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\beta^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}g},

where the error term
\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}g}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid X_{Pdd^{\prime}_{g}}]=0
. The vector of coefficients
(\alpha^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\beta^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}})^{\prime}
is estimated by

\displaystyle\big(\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\hat{\beta}^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}=\big(\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widetilde{Y}_{idd^{\prime}},

where
\widetilde{Y}_{idd^{\prime}}
is defined as a
G\times 1
vector with the
g
-th element as
Y_{ig}\mathbbm{1}\big\{D_{0g}=d\big\}\mathbbm{1}\big\{D_{1g}=d^{\prime}\big\}
, and
\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}
is obtained by substituting
\widehat{P}_{0g}
,
\widehat{P}_{1g}
, and
\hat{\rho}
for the true values into
X_{Pdd^{\prime}}
. Then, we can write the estimated coefficients as

\displaystyle\big(\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\hat{\beta}^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\big(X_{Pdd^{\prime}}\big(\alpha^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\beta^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}\big(\alpha^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\beta^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}+\big(\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}.

Let
\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}=X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\Delta_{G}
, where
\Delta_{G}
is defined as a
G\times K
matrix such that
\Delta_{G}=\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}-X_{Pdd^{\prime}}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\big(X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\Delta_{G}\big)^{\prime}\big(X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\Delta_{G}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{G}X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\Delta_{G}+\frac{1}{G}\Delta_{G}^{\prime}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{G}\Delta_{G}^{\prime}\Delta_{G},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\big(X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\Delta_{G}\big)^{\prime}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{G}\Delta_{G}^{\prime}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities, we obtain

\frac{1}{G}\big\|X_{Pdd^{\prime}}\Delta_{G}\big\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\|X_{Pdd^{\prime}}\|_{F}^{2}}{G}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\|\Delta_{G}\|_{F}^{2}}{G}}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0,

since
\|\Delta_{G}\|_{F}^{2}/G\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
, and
\|X_{Pdd^{\prime}}\|_{F}^{2}/G
is bounded almost surely by
\mathbb{E}[X_{Pdd^{\prime}}^{\prime},X_{Pdd^{\prime}}]
is nonsingular. Therefore, we should have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{G}X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+o_{a.s.}(1)\overset{a.s.}{\to}\mathbb{E}\big[X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}g}X_{Pdd^{\prime}g}\big],

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{G}X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+o_{a.s.}(1)\overset{a.s.}{\to}\mathbb{E}\big[X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}g}X_{Pdd^{\prime}g}\big],

and then

\bigg(\frac{1}{G}\widehat{X}^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}\bigg)^{-1}\overset{a.s.}{\to}\bigg(\mathbb{E}\big[X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}g}X_{Pdd^{\prime}g}\big]\bigg)^{-1}

by the continuous mapping theorem and the nonsingularity condition.

We can also express the term
\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}/G
as

\frac{\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}}{G}=\frac{1}{G}\big(X_{Pdd^{\prime}}+\Delta_{G}\big)^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{G}X_{Pdd^{\prime}}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{G}\Delta_{G}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}.

The first term
X_{Pdd^{\prime}}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}/G\overset{a.s.}{\to}\mathbb{E}[X_{Pdd^{\prime}_{g}}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}g}]=0
as
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}g}\mid X_{Pdd^{\prime}_{g}}]=0
. Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz, the second term becomes

\bigg\|\frac{1}{G}\Delta_{G}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}\bigg\|\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{G}\big\|\Delta_{G}\big\|_{F}^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{G}\|\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}\|^{2}}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0,

since
\|\Delta_{G}\|_{F}^{2}/G\overset{a.s.}{\to}0
, and
\|\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}\|^{2}/G
is bounded almost surely by
\text{Var}(\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}g})=\sigma_{idd^{\prime}g}<\infty
. Thus,

\frac{\widehat{X}_{Pdd^{\prime}}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{idd^{\prime}}}{G}\overset{a.s.}{\to}0.

Combining the above results, we have

\big(\hat{\alpha}^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\hat{\beta}^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}\overset{a.s.}{\to}\bigg(\mathbb{E}\big[X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}g}X_{Pdd^{\prime}g}\big]\bigg)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big[X^{\prime}_{Pdd^{\prime}g}X_{Pdd^{\prime}g}\big]\big(\alpha^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\beta^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}=\big(\alpha^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}},\beta^{\prime}_{idd^{\prime}}\big)^{\prime}.

Appendix I
Model With Continuous Treatment

I.1
Setting

An important extension of Section
2
considers settings in which the treatment
D_{ig}
is continuous. This extension broadens the applicability of our framework to a wide range of empirical environments. For example, in education, students’ long-term outcomes may depend not only on their own time of schooling or study intensity but also on the continuous education investments of their peers, such as best friends or classmates. Similarly, in agriculture, a farmer’s yield may be influenced by her own input choices, such as fertilizer use or irrigation intensity, as well as by neighboring farmers’ continuous decisions that affect shared resources such as groundwater or pest control. In both cases, treatment choices are endogenous, depending on unobserved preferences, constraints, or abilities, while outcomes may be directly affected by peers’ continuous treatments within the group.

For expositional clarity, we focus on the case where each group contains two units,
i=0,1
, while the identification results extend straightforwardly to settings with any finite number of units per group. In this extension, the treatment for unit
i
in group
g
,
D_{ig}\in\mathbb{R}
, is a continuous random variable. We formulate the following potential outcomes framework To accommodate such treatments. Again, we suppress the group subscript
g
for notational convenience.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{i}=g_{i}(D_{i},D_{-i},U_{i},U_{-i})\\
D_{i}=h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i},V_{i})\end{array}\right.

(28)

In Equation (
28
), the random variable
V_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
represents private unobserved characteristics, such as ability in education decisions or resource constraints in agricultural production, that may simultaneously influence both the treatment
D_{i}
and the outcome
Y_{i}
, thereby generating endogeneity. To address this endogeneity, we introduce continuous instrumental variables. Specifically, each unit
i
is assigned a vector of instruments
Z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{i}}
. We allow the endogenous treatment
D_{i}
to depend not only on the individual’s own instrument
Z_{i}
but also on her peer’s instrument
Z_{-i}
. This formulation accommodates the possibility of spillovers in treatment assignment, where one individual’s instruments may affect both her own treatment decision and those of her peers.

In the outcome equation, we allow the individual’s outcome
Y_{i}
to depend not only on her own continuous treatment
D_{i}
but also on the vector of continuous treatments
D_{-i}
chosen by her peers within the group. The random vector
U_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{l_{i}}
captures the individual-specific unobserved characteristics that affect outcomes, and we impose no restrictions on its dimensionality. Moreover, we explicitly permit the outcome
Y_{i}
to depend on the peers’ unobservables
U_{-i}
. This formulation captures a rich set of spillover channels, as outcomes may be influenced both by peers’ observed treatment decisions and by their latent characteristics. For example, in education, a student’s earnings or academic performance may depend on both her own study effort and her peers’ corresponding education investments. At the same time, peers’ unobserved abilities or motivation may also affect individual’s outcomes through collaboration, competition, or shared environments.

For the unobserved random variables
(U_{i},V_{i})
, we impose no restrictions on their joint dependence structure with
(U_{-i},V_{-i})
within each group, similar to the discrete treatment case. This allows for arbitrary correlation in both outcome- and treatment-related unobservables across group members.

In the continuous treatment setting, we maintain Assumption
3
and introduce two additional conditions, Assumptions
17
and
18
, to establish identification of the marginal controlled spillover and direct effects.

Assumption 17
.

(Random assignment: continuous treatment)
We assume that the instruments assigned to each group,
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})
, satisfy

\big(Z_{i},Z_{-i}\big)\perp\!\!\!\perp\big(V_{i},V_{-i},U_{i},U_{-i}\big).

Assumption 18
.

(Monotonicity of
h_{i}
)
Given
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, the treatment function
h_{i}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}},v)
is continuous and strictly monotonic in
v
.

In Assumption
17
, we require that the instruments are randomly assigned at the group level. Assumption
18
further imposes a monotonicity condition on the function
h_{i}(\cdot)
, ensuring that the treatment
D_{i}
is uniquely mapped to the unobservable
V_{i}
conditional on the instruments
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})
. Formally, given
Z_{i}=\mathbf{z}
and
Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}}
, the equation
D_{i}\mid(Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}})=h_{i}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}},V_{i})
can be inverted with respect to
V_{i}
, yielding
V_{i}\mid(Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}})={h^{i}_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}}}}^{-1}(D_{i})
. Then, under Assumption
17
, it follows that
V_{i}={h^{i}_{Z_{i},Z_{-i}}}^{-1}(D_{i})
. Without loss of generality, we specify that
h_{i}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}},v)
is strictly increasing in
v
for all instrument values
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}})
. The monotonicity requirement on the treatment function
h_{i}
is not an additional structural restriction but instead a natural implication of the existence of a well-defined conditional distribution of the treatment. As shown in
18
, if the conditional distribution
F_{D_{i}\mid Z_{i},Z_{-i}}
is strictly increasing and continuous, then the condition
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})\perp\!\!\!\perp V_{i}
, together with Assumptions
3
and
18
, follows naturally under a reduced-form interpretation of the treatment selection equation. In particular, by defining
h_{i}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}},v)=Q_{D_{i}\mid Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}}}(v)
and
V_{i}=F_{D_{i}\mid Z_{i},Z_{-i}}(D_{i})
, where
Q_{D_{i}\mid Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}}}
denotes the quantile function of distribution
D_{i}\mid Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}}
, the treatment
D_{i}
can be represented as a strictly increasing function of the latent variable
V_{i}
.

I.2
Identification

Analogous to the discrete-treatment case in Equation (
16
), we define the propensity score function under continuous treatments as
\mathbb{P}\left(D_{i}\leq d\mid Z_{i},Z_{-i}\right)\equiv P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i},d)
given
d\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle P_{i}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}},d)\equiv

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(D_{i}\leq d\mid Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}}\right)

(29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(h(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}},V_{i})\leq d\mid Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq{h^{i}_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}}}}^{-1}(d)\mid Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{i}\leq{h^{i}_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}}}}^{-1}(d)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle h^{i}_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}}}}^{-1}(d),

where the second equality in Equation (
29
) is a direct implication of Assumption
18
, which guarantees invertibility of the treatment equation. The third equality holds under Assumption
17
, requiring random assignment of instruments. Finally, the last equality is implied by Assumption
3
, since the unobservable
V_{i}
can be normalized to follow a uniform distribution on
[0,1]
. Denote the identified support of the propensity score
P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i},D_{i})
as
\mathcal{P}_{i}
.

After identifying the mapping
{h^{i}_{Z_{i},Z_{-i}}}^{-1}(D_{i})
for
i\in\{0,1\}
within each group
g
, we can identify the conditional distributions of potential outcomes by using the propensity score functions
P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i},D_{i})
as control functions:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{i}\in B\mid D_{i}=d,D_{-i}=d^{\prime},P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i},D_{i})=p,P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i},D_{-i})=p^{\prime}\right)

(30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(g_{i}(d,d^{\prime},U_{i})\in B\mid h_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i},V_{i})=d,h_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i},V_{-i})=d^{\prime},\right.

\displaystyle\left.{h^{i}_{Z_{i},Z_{-i}}}^{-1}(d)=p,{h^{-i}_{Z_{-i},Z_{i}}}^{-1}(d^{\prime})=p^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(g_{i}(d,d^{\prime},U_{i})\in B\mid V_{i}={h^{i}_{Z_{i},Z_{-i}}}^{-1}(d),V_{-i}={h^{-i}_{Z_{-i},Z_{i}}}^{-1}(d^{\prime}),\right.

\displaystyle\left.{h^{i}_{Z_{i},Z_{-i}}}^{-1}(d)=p,{h^{-i}_{Z_{-i},Z_{i}}}^{-1}(d^{\prime})=p^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(g(d,d^{\prime},U_{i})\in B\mid V_{i}=p,V_{-i}=p^{\prime},{h^{i}_{Z_{i},Z_{-i}}}^{-1}(d)=p,{h^{-i}_{Z_{-i},Z_{i}}}^{-1}(d^{\prime})=p^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime})\in B\mid V_{i}=p,V_{-i}=p^{\prime}\right),

where
(d,d^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
(p,p^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\times\mathcal{P}_{-i}
, and
B
denotes any Borel set in the sigma-field generated by
Y_{i}
. The first equality follows directly from Equation (
29
), which links the propensity score to the inverse of the treatment function. The second equality is implied by Assumption
18
, which ensures that the treatment equation is strictly monotone in the unobservable and hence invertible. Finally, the last equality holds under Assumption
17
, since the instruments
(Z_{i},Z_{-i})
are independent of the unobserved component
U_{i}
.

Equation (
30
) provides identification of the marginal treatment response (MTR) function,

m_{i}^{(d,d^{\prime})}(p,p^{\prime})\equiv\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}\left(d,d^{\prime}\right)\mid V_{i}=p,V_{-i}=p^{\prime}\big],(d,d^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},(p,p^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\times\mathcal{P}_{-i},

by taking expectations over the identified potential outcome distributions,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}(d,d^{\prime})\in B\mid V_{i}=p,V_{-i}=p^{\prime})
. Building on this result, the marginal controlled spillover effect (MCSE) and the marginal controlled direct effect (MCDE) with continuous treatments are identified as differences across the MTR functions, corresponding respectively to changes in peers’ treatments and in one’s own treatment while conditioning on latent characteristics
(V_{i},V_{-i})
.

Theorem 6
.

(Identifying marginal treatment response)
Consider the model in Equation (
28
). Suppose that Assumptions
3
,
17
and
18
hold. For any
d_{0},d_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
(p_{0},p_{1})\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\times\mathcal{P}_{-i}
,
m_{i}^{(d_{0},d_{1})}(p_{0},p_{1})
is identified as

\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}\mid D_{i}=d_{0},D_{-i}=d_{1},P_{i}(Z_{i},Z_{-i},D_{i})=p_{0},P_{-i}(Z_{-i},Z_{i},D_{-i})=p_{1}\big],

where
P_{i}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z^{\prime}},d)\equiv\mathbb{P}(D_{i}\leq d\mid Z_{i}=\mathbf{z},Z_{-i}=\mathbf{z^{\prime}})
.

Appendix J
Proofs for the Exposure Mapping Model

The individual propensity score
P_{ig}(Z_{g})
can be used to identify her threshold function
h_{i}(\cdot)
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(D_{ig}=1\mid Z_{g}=z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{ig}\leq h_{i}\left(Z_{g}\right)\mid Z_{g}=z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{ig}\leq h_{i}\left(z\right)\mid Z_{g}=z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{ig}\leq h_{i}\left(z\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{i}\left(z\right).

These equalities follow from two key ingredients. First, by normalizing the individual unobservable
V_{ig}
to be uniformly distributed on
(0,1)
. Second, because the group-level instrument
Z_{g}
is randomly assigned, it is independent of the unobserved heterogeneity. Additionally, the group-level propensity score function identifies the inverse of the exposure function
m
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(H_{g}\leq h\mid Z_{g}=z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(m\left(z,\varepsilon_{g}\right)\leq h\mid Z_{g}=z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\varepsilon_{g}\leq m^{-1}_{z}(h)\mid Z_{g}=z\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\varepsilon_{g}\leq m^{-1}_{z}(h)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{-1}_{z}(h).

The second equality is implied by Assumption
15
, which guarantees that the exposure mapping is strictly monotone in the group-level unobservable and therefore invertible. The third equality follows from Assumption
14
, which ensures that the instruments are randomly assigned and thus independent of group-level unobserved heterogeneity. Finally, the last equality results from normalizing the group-level unobservable
\varepsilon_{g}
to follow a uniform distribution on
(0,1)
.

Next, we use the individual- and group-level propensity score functions as control functions to identify the conditional distribution of
V_{ig}
given
\varepsilon_{g}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(D_{ig}=1\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}(Z_{g})=p_{0},P_{g}(Z_{g},H_{g})=p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{ig}\leq h_{i}(Z_{g})\mid m(Z_{g},\varepsilon_{g})=h,h_{i}(Z_{g})=p_{0},m_{Z_{g}}^{-1}(h)=p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{ig}\leq p_{0}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=m_{Z_{g}}^{-1}(h),h_{i}(Z_{g})=p_{0},m_{Z_{g}}^{-1}(h)=p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{ig}\leq p_{0}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1},h_{i}(Z_{g})=p_{0},m_{Z_{g}}^{-1}(h)=p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V_{ig}\leq p_{0}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}\right),

where the first equality follows from the identification of control functions, the second equality is implied by the monotonicity of function
m(\cdot)
, and the last equality holds under the random assignment of instruments
Z_{g}
.

Given that the probability
\mathbb{P}(D_{ig}=1\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}(Z_{g})=p_{0},P_{g}(Z_{g},H_{g})=p_{1})
is differentiable with respect to
p_{0}
, we can take derivatives to recover the conditional density of the individual-level unobservable given the group-level unobservable, denoted
f_{V_{ig}\mid\varepsilon_{g}}(\cdot)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}\left(D_{ig}=1\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}(Z_{g})=p_{0},P_{g}(Z_{g},H_{g})=p_{1}\right)=f_{V_{ig}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}}(p_{0}).

Then, the marginal treatment response function,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{ig}(1,h)\mid V_{ig}=p_{0},\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}]
, can be identified from

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{ig}D_{ig}\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}\left(Z_{g}\right)=p_{0},P_{g}\left(Z_{g},H_{g}\right)=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{ig}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{ig}\leq h_{i}(Z_{g})\}\mid m(Z_{g},\varepsilon_{g})=h,h_{i}(Z_{g})=p_{0},m_{Z_{g}}^{-1}(h)=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{ig}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{ig}\leq p_{0}\}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=m_{Z_{g}}^{-1}(h),h_{i}(Z_{g})=p_{0},m_{Z_{g}}^{-1}(h)=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{ig}\mathbbm{1}\{V_{ig}\leq p_{0}\}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}\right].

Given that the function
\mathbb{E}[Y_{ig}D_{ig}\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}\left(Z_{g}\right)=p_{0},P_{g}\left(Z_{g},H_{g}\right)=p_{1}]
is differentiable with repect to
p_{0}
and that the marginal treatment response functions are continuous, we can differentiate this conditional expectation to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{ig}D_{ig}\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}\left(Z_{g}\right)=p_{0},P_{g}\left(Z_{g},H_{g}\right)=p_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{ig}(1,h)\mid V_{ig}=p_{0},\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}\right]\cdot f_{V_{ig}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}}(p_{0}).

By dividing both sides of the previous expression by the conditional density
f_{V_{ig}\mid\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}}(p_{0})
, we can identify
\mathbb{E}[Y_{ig}(1,h)\mid V_{ig}=p_{0},\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}]
for
h\in\mathbb{R}
and
(p_{0},p_{1})\in\mathcal{P}
. Analogously, the marginal treatment response function
\mathbb{E}[Y_{ig}(0,h)\mid V_{ig}=p_{0},\varepsilon_{g}=p_{1}]
can be identified as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{ig}(1-D_{ig})\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}\left(Z_{g}\right)=p_{0},P_{g}\left(Z_{g},H_{g}\right)=p_{1}\right]\Big/

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{0}}\mathbb{P}\left(1-D_{ig}\mid H_{g}=h,P_{ig}\left(Z_{g}\right)=p_{0},P_{g}\left(Z_{g},H_{g}\right)=p_{1}\right).
